The **Albanese government** is the sitting [federal government of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Australia), led by Prime Minister [Anthony Albanese](556745.md) of the [Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party). The Albanese government was sworn in on 23 May 2022 by the [governor-general of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-general_of_Australia), [David Hurley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hurley).[^cite_note-1] The party governed with a two-to-three seat majority in the [House of Representatives](61565.md) in its first term,[^cite_note-2] and expanded its majority to 19 seats in its second term. It won a plurality of seats in the Senate at the [2025 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Australian_federal_election), though has never held a majority of seats in the upper house.

Albanese succeeded the [Morrison government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morrison_government) after the [Liberal–National Coalition](1051313.md) was defeated at the [2022 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Australian_federal_election), and saw the first Labor government to be elected at the federal level since the [Rudd government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudd_government_\(2013\)) was defeated at the [2013 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Australian_federal_election). The Albanese government went on win a [landslide victory](41053306.md) in the [2025 federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Australian_federal_election), making Albanese the first Prime Minister to serve a full term and win another since [John Howard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Howard)'s victory at the [2004 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Australian_federal_election).[^cite_note-3]

## Background

### 2022 federal election

Main article: [2022 Australian federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Australian_federal_election)

The 2022 election was called by Morrison on 10 April 2022, when he visited the [governor-general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia), advising the latter to prorogue Parliament and dissolve the House of Representatives. The governor-general accepted Morrison's recommendations, as is the custom in Australia's Westminster system of government.[^cite_note-timing101-4][^cite_note-5] The Parliament was then prorogued and the House of Representatives dissolved the next morning. The Labor election campaign focused on [aged care in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aged_care_in_Australia), the introduction of a [National Anti-Corruption Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Anti-Corruption_Commission_\(Australia\)), childcare subsidies, climate change, a similar defence budget to the Coalition but with a Defence Posture Review into resources and strategy, education, electric vehicles, farming, health, housing, infrastructure, a review of the [National Disability Insurance Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Disability_Insurance_Scheme) (NDIS), as well as measures to help older Australians.[^cite_note-6]

The election was held on 21 May 2022.[^cite_note-green_date-7] As of 10\:00PM AEST on election night, the [Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party) (led by [Anthony Albanese](556745.md)) was projected to form a government by [ABC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_\(Australia\)), although it was not clear whether they would have a majority or a minority.[^cite_note-8] Notable outcomes included the popularity of community independents in several inner-city seats, costing Treasurer and Deputy Liberal Leader [Josh Frydenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josh_Frydenberg) his seat; a particularly sizable swing from the Coalition to Labor in Western Australia; and notably strong support for the [Australian Greens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Greens) in some inner-city Brisbane seats. After the bulk of the votes had been counted and a Labor victory appeared inevitable, Morrison conceded the election, and then announced his intention to resign as the Liberal Party leader.[^cite_note-9] The Coalition's loss was attributed to Morrison's unpopularity with voters and the popularity of centrist "[teal independents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teal_independents)" in certain inner-city electorates.[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-auto2-11] Albanese, who also made history as the first Italian-Australian to secure the position of Prime Minister,[^cite_note-12] was sworn in as the new Prime Minister of Australia on 23 May 2022.[^cite_note-13]

#### 2023 by-election

On 9 February 2023, former Liberal Party cabinet minister [Alan Tudge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Tudge) resigned from Parliament, triggering a [by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Aston_by-election) in his seat of [Aston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_Aston).[^cite_note-14] In an unexpected result, Labor's [Mary Doyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Doyle) won the by-election, marking the first time a governing party won a by-election against the opposition since 1920.[^cite_note-15] As a result of this, Albanese's government increased their majority in the House of Representatives to 78 of 151 seats.

## Appointments

See also: [First Albanese ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Albanese_ministry) and [Second Albanese ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Albanese_ministry)

### Interim Ministry (May 2022)

Although it was not certain on election night that Labor would win a majority, no other party could realistically form a government. Accordingly, two days after the election, Albanese, deputy leader [Richard Marles](4386805.md), shadow treasurer [Jim Chalmers](40746506.md), and senators [Penny Wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Wong) and [Katy Gallagher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katy_Gallagher) were sworn in as an interim five-person ministry. The five ministers divided all portfolios between them until the full ministry was determined. According to Australia's [ABC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_\(Australia\)), the governor-general [David Hurley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hurley) would not have sworn in Albanese without assurances that Labor could provide stable government, as well as legal advice that this was the proper course of action. Albanese confirmed that he secured confidence and supply from the crossbench in the event that he was not able to form majority government.[^cite_note-16]

### Full Ministry (June 2022 onwards)

Albanese announced the composition of his full [first ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Albanese_ministry) on 31 May 2022. Given that frontbenchers [Kristina Keneally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristina_Keneally) and [Terri Butler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terri_Butler) were not re-elected, [Murray Watt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_Watt) and [Clare O’Neil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clare_O'Neil) were chosen by the Labor caucus to replace them in the cabinet. With 19 female frontbenchers and 10 female cabinet ministers, it became the most gender-equal ministry in Australian history.[^cite_note-17] The full ministry was sworn in by [Governor-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia) [David Hurley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hurley) on 1 June 2022.[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19]

Albanese announced a [reshuffle](845103.md) of his Ministry on 28 July 2024 due to the impending retirements of [Linda Burney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Burney) and [Brendan O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brendan_O'Connor_\(politician\)). Senator [Malarndirri McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malarndirri_McCarthy) succeeded Burney as the Minister for Indigenous affairs while [Andrew Giles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Giles) assumed the Skills and Training portfolio. [Clare O'Neil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clare_O'Neil) also assumed the housing portfolio.[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21]

Following the [2025 Australian federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Australian_federal_election) held on 3 May 2025, a cabinet reshuffle occurred in mid-May. While Albanese, [Richard Marles](4386805.md), [Jim Chalmers](40746506.md), [Katy Gallagher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katy_Gallagher), [Penny Wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Wong) and [Don Farrell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Farrell)'s portfolios remained unchanged in the [Second Albanese ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Albanese_ministry), [Michelle Rowland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Rowland) was appointed as [Attorney-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Australia), [Tim Ayres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Ayres) was appointed as [Minister for Industry and Innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Industry_and_Innovation), [Anika Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anika_Wells) as [Minister for Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Communications_\(Australia\)) and [Sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Sport_\(Australia\)), [Amanda Rishworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanda_Rishworth) as [Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Employment_and_Workplace_Relations) and [Anne Aly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne_Aly) as [Minister for Small Business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Small_Business_\(Australia\)). In addition, [Daniel Mulino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Mulino) was appointed as [Assistant Treasurer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assistant_Treasurer_of_Australia), [Jess Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jess_Walsh) as [Minister for Early Childhood Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Education_\(Australia\)), [Andrew Charlton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Charlton) as [Cabinet Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_Secretary_\(Australian_minister\)), [Jenny McAllister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jenny_McAllister) as [Minister for NDIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Disability_and_the_National_Disability_Insurance_Scheme), [Tanya Plibersek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanya_Plibersek) as [Minister for Social Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Social_Services), [Murray Watt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_Watt) as [Minister for the Environment and Water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_the_Environment_and_Water) and [Sam Rae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Rae) as [Minister for Aged Care and Seniors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Aged_Care_and_Seniors).[^cite_note-22]

### Public service

[Phil Gaetjens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phil_Gaetjens), the [Secretary of the Department of Prime Minister and Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_the_Department_of_the_Prime_Minister_and_Cabinet_\(Australia\)) under [Scott Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Morrison), took leave the day before the Albanese government was sworn in. This had been expected given Albanese had indicated he would not want Gaetjens to continue in the role.[^cite_note-23] On 6 June 2022, former [University of Melbourne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Melbourne) vice-chancellor [Glyn Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glyn_Davis) replaced Gaetjens.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] In June 2024, the government announced that [Matt Kean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Kean), a former Liberal [Treasurer of New South Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treasurer_of_New_South_Wales), would be appointed to lead the [Climate Change Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Change_Authority).[^cite_note-26]

### Judiciary

On 17 October 2022, the government appointed [Jayne Jagot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayne_Jagot) as a Justice of the [High Court of Australia](314201.md), replacing the retiring Justice [Patrick Keane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Keane). On Jagot's appointment, the [High Court of Australia](314201.md) had a majority of female Justices for the first time in its history.[^cite_note-27] On 6 November 2023, the government appointed [Robert Beech-Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Beech-Jones) as a High Court Justice. Beech-Jones replaced [Stephen Gageler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Gageler), who was made the [Chief Justice of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_Australia) upon the retirement of [Susan Kiefel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Kiefel). These appointments ended the brief female majority on the court.[^cite_note-28]

### Ambassadorships

On 30 September 2022, the government announced that it had nominated former Defence Minister [Stephen Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Smith_\(Australian_politician\)) as the next [High Commissioner of Australia to the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_commissioners_of_Australia_to_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-29] Smith commenced his position in January 2023.[^cite_note-30] On 20 December 2022, the government announced that it had nominated former prime minister [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) as the next [Ambassador of Australia to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambassador_of_Australia_to_the_United_States).[^cite_note-31] Rudd commenced his position in March 2023.[^cite_note-32]

### Governor-General

On 3 April 2024, the prime minister announced that [King Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Charles_III) had approved Albanese's appointment of [Sam Mostyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Mostyn) to succeed General [David Hurley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hurley) as [Governor-General of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia).[^cite_note-33] Mostyn commenced her position on 1 July 2024.[^cite_note-34]

### Government officials

In July 2024, owing to the rise in [antisemitic activity in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_Australia) following the [October 7 attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_7_attack) in [Israel](9282173.md), Albanese appointed [Jillian Segal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jillian_Segal) as Australian special envoy on combatting [antisemitism](1078.md).[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] Segal has a long history of advocacy for Jewish communities and combating antisemitism, and her appointment was supported by the federal opposition.[^cite_note-Kelly_2024-37][^cite_note-38] In October 2024, Albanese appointed Malik as Australian special envoy on combatting [Islamophobia](162173.md). Malik is a prominent Muslim leader and advocate for interfaith dialogue.[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40]

## First term of government (2022–2025)

See also: [47th Parliament of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/47th_Parliament_of_Australia) and [First Albanese ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Albanese_ministry)

### Economy

Jim Chalmers

Katy Gallagher

[Jim Chalmers](40746506.md) (current [Treasurer](579169.md)) and [Katy Gallagher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katy_Gallagher) (current [Finance Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Finance_\(Australia\))).

[Jim Chalmers](40746506.md) was sworn in as [Treasurer of Australia](579169.md) and [Katy Gallagher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katy_Gallagher) was sworn in as [Finance Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Finance_\(Australia\)) as part of the interim Albanese ministry. The Albanese government commenced amidst the [2021–23 global surge in inflation](69605317.md). The Australian inflation rate peaked at 7.5% at the end of 2022, a 32-year high, before easing to 2.8% by October 2024.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42] Between 4 May 2022 and 8 November 2023, the [Reserve Bank of Australia](492355.md) steadily raised interest rates from 0.10% to 4.35%, a 12-year high.[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44] The Bank then maintained the rate of 4.35% until cutting it to 4.10% on 19 February 2025, the first rate cut since March 2020.[^cite_note-45]

#### Tax cuts and reform

On 28 February 2023, the government announced that it would seek to reduce tax concessions on superannuation accounts with more than \$3 million.[^cite_note-46] In November 2023 it introduced legislation to do this, but was unable to secure support for it from the Senate.[^cite_note-47]

In January 2024, the Albanese government announced a restructuring of the "Stage Three" tax cuts that had been passed by the previous government but had not yet come into effect.[^cite_note-48] The Albanese government's restructuring gave greater tax benefits to those earning under \$150,000 whilst reducing the original tax cut for higher earners.[^cite_note-\:0-49][^cite_note-50] The Coalition criticised Albanese for breaking a promise not to change the cuts and initially suggested they would not support it,[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-\:2-52] but did eventually support the legislation's passage in February 2024.[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] The modified tax cuts came into effect on 1 July 2024.[^cite_note-55]

On 27 March 2025, as part of its [fourth budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Australian_federal_budget), the Albanese government legislated a further tax cut that Chalmers called "modest but meaningful".[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57] This tax cut will reduce the 16% rate for the lowest tax bracket (\$18,201 and \$45,000) for all taxpayers, down to 15% on 1 July 2026 and then to 14% on 1 July 2027.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59]

#### Budgets

Early in the government's term, Treasurer Chalmers confirmed that the new government would hand down a revised budget in October 2022.[^cite_note-auto-60] In the lead up to the October budget, Chalmers and Gallagher launched an audit to highlight any waste left behind by the previous government.[^cite_note-auto-60] Secretary of the Department of Treasury [Steven Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Kennedy) noted that the budget needed to be brought under control and that the tax system needed to be made fit for use.[^cite_note-61] On 25 October 2022, Chalmers handed down a [revised budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2022_Australian_federal_budget) — the first under the Albanese government. The budget forecast that inflation would peak at 7.75% at the end of the year. The budget also took steps to fund the government's election promises largely by using funding earmarked by the former government which had not yet been spent.[^cite_note-62]

In May 2023, Chalmers handed down the government's [second budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Australian_federal_budget). The budget delivered a surplus of \$22.1 billion (equivalent to 0.9% of Australia's GDP), which was well above the government's forecasted surplus of \$4.3 billion; this was Australia's first budget surplus in 15 years, and the largest ever Australian budget surplus.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] In May 2024, Chalmers handed down the government's [third budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Australian_federal_budget). This budget delivered a surplus of \$9.3 billion, making it the first consecutive surplus in an Australian federal budget since 2007–08.[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] In May 2025, Chalmers handed down the government's [fourth budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Australian_federal_budget).[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73]

#### "Future Made in Australia" manufacturing policy

In April 2024, Prime Minister Albanese announced a major industrial policy called "[Future Made in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_Made_in_Australia)", which seeks to promote Australian manufacturing in [sustainable energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_energy).[^cite_note-74] As part of the policy, the government's third budget contained \$22.7 billion over a decade in support of domestic [green hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_hydrogen), solar-panel manufacturing, and mining of critical minerals.[^cite_note-75] This includes a \$1 billion "Solar Sunshot" program to support solar panel manufacturing in Australia,[^cite_note-76] and a \$566 million "Resourcing Australia's Prosperity" initiative for geomapping for mining resources.[^cite_note-77] In July 2024, the Albanese government introduced legislation to give further effect to the policy,[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79] which passed the Parliament in November 2024.[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81] As part of the Parliamentary negotiations, \$500 million for the electrification of social housing was added to the scheme.[^cite_note-82]

### Industrial relations and employment



[Tony Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Burke), [Minister for Employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Employment,_Workforce,_Skills,_Small_and_Family_Business) from 2022 to 2024 and current [Leader of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_\(Australia\))

[Tony Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Burke) served as [Minister for Employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Employment,_Workforce,_Skills,_Small_and_Family_Business) from 2022 to 2024, succeeded by Senator [Murray Watt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_Watt) after a July 2024 reshuffle of Cabinet.[^cite_note-83] On 3 June 2022, Minister Burke confirmed that the government had made a formal submission to the [Fair Work Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Work_Commission) in support of an increase to the minimum wage.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85] Subsequently, on 15 June 2022, the Fair Work Commission announced that the minimum wage would be raised by 5.2%.[^cite_note-86] In September 2022, in fulfilment of a campaign promise, Albanese held a "Jobs and Skills Summit" with unions and business leaders.[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88] It resulted in the government agreeing to 36 initiatives involving education, housing, workplace relations, and immigration.[^cite_note-89]

On 28 November 2022, the government passed new workplace harassment laws through the Parliament. These laws implement the recommendation of the Respect\@Work Report to create a positive duty requiring employers to implement measures to prevent sexual harassment, sex discrimination, and victimisation.[^cite_note-90] On 2 December 2022, the government passed its *Secure Jobs, Better Pay* law through the Parliament. The new law allows unions to negotiate multi-employer pay deals in an effort to secure wage increases across particular sectors such as child care and aged care. The law also aims to close the gender pay gap by prohibiting pay secrecy employment clauses and secures the right of workers to seek flexible working arrangements.[^cite_note-91]

Throughout 2023, the Albanese government attempted to pass additional industrial relations reforms through Parliament, dubbed the *Closing Loopholes* bill, which aimed to ensure temporary workers employed through [labour hire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_hire_\(Australia\)) were paid the same wage as regular workers, criminalise [wage theft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wage_theft), and make companies responsible for [industrial manslaughter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_manslaughter), among other changes.[^cite_note-92] Despite opposition from the Liberal Party and business lobbies, the first part of the bill was passed by the Senate on 7 December 2023.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] The second tranche of legislation, which introduced minimum standards for [gig workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gig_worker), was passed on 8 February 2024.[^cite_note-95]

On 13 December 2023, Australian federal and state workplace ministers agreed to ban the use, supply, and manufacturing of [engineered stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engineered_stone) from 1 July 2024. This initiative followed a [Safe Work Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safe_Work_Australia) report that found the rates of [silicosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicosis) and silica-related diseases had risen substantially particularly among engineered stone workers.[^cite_note-96] In mid-January 2024, Burke declined to intervene in an industrial dispute between the [Australian Maritime Officers Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Maritime_Officers_Union) and [DP World](3272467.md) but criticised the latter for allegedly acting in "bad faith".[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98] In its May 2024 budget, the government announced that superannuation would be added to Commonwealth-funded paid parental leave from 1 July 2025.[^cite_note-99] In late August 2024, the federal government's [right to disconnect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_disconnect) laws came into effect. This allows employees to ignore calls or messages from their employers outside of work hours, except where such refusal is unreasonable.[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101]

### Immigration



[Clare O'Neil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clare_O'Neil), [Minister for Home Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Home_Affairs_\(Australia\)) from 2022 to 2024

[Clare O'Neil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clare_O'Neil) was appointed [Minister for Home Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Home_Affairs_\(Australia\)).[^cite_note-102] Prior to her appointment, on 27 May 2022, interim Minister [Jim Chalmers](40746506.md) announced that he had exercised his power to allow the [Murugappan family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murugappan_family_asylum_claims) to return home to [Biloela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biloela) on bridging visas.[^cite_note-103] In September 2022, the Albanese government increased the permanent migration intake from 160,000 to a record 195,000 a year.[^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] Net overseas migration reached 536,000 in the 2022–23 financial year, the highest in Australian history, which the Albanese government attributed to being a "catch-up" on the migration decrease during the [COVID-19 border closures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Australia) in Australia.[^cite_note-107] Net overseas migration then decreased to 446,000 in the 2023–24 financial year, and is estimated to be 340,000 over the 2024–25 financial year.[^cite_note-108] Net permanent and long-term arrivals in the 12 months to May 2025 were 447,620.[^cite_note-109]

In October 2022, the Albanese government repatriated four Australian-citizen [ISIS brides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISIS_brides) and their 13 children from Syria to western Sydney,[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111] which some local mayors and representative [Dai Le](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dai_Le) criticised.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] As of June 2024, the government has not repatriated any further ISIS brides or their children.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115] In the leadup to the 2022 election, Labor promised that it would abolish temporary protection visas. It did so by early 2023, allowing 19,000 asylum seekers who had been living in Australia for a decade to gain permanent residency.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117]

In November 2023, the High Court held that indefinite immigration detention is unlawful.[^cite_note-118] In response to this ruling and demands from those indefinitely detained, the Albanese government began releasing people clearly impacted by the case.[^cite_note-119] Within a week, 80 people were released, with Giles saying that all are on visa conditions including reporting.[^cite_note-120] The Albanese government is seeking to clarify if the government must release people from immigration detention if they have refused to cooperate with attempts to deport them.[^cite_note-121] The government is seeking to enact legislation that will jail immigration detainees and unlawful non-citizens for a minimum of one year if they do not cooperate with attempts to deport them.[^cite_note-122] In December 2023, the Albanese government released its ten-year [migration](1100670.md#Migration_program) strategy which includes increasing minimum English language requirements for international students and tightening visa processes for migrant workers.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124]

According to some critics, the [housing crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_property_market) in Australia was exacerbated by the immigration policies of the Albanese government.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126]

#### International student migration

In January 2024, the government signalled changes that would make it easier for international students to settle permanently in Australia.[^cite_note-127] However, on 1 July 2024, the government enacted policy changes intended to limit the available of visas for international student graduates. These changes included excluding students aged over 35 years from the Temporary Graduate visa scheme, reducing the time students are permitted to remain and work in Australia after completing their tertiary studies, increasing financial capacity requirements, and increasing English language requirements.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

On 1 July 2024, the government announced plans to increase the fee for international student visas from \$710 to \$1600.[^cite_note-130] Representatives from the university sector expressed concern that the increased fee would deter international students from studying in Australia.[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] On 27 August 2024, the Australian Government announced plans to limit the number of international student tertiary enrolments to 270,000, with 145,000 enrolments allocated to university enrolments.[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134] On 6 September 2024, the government announced the maximum number of student enrolments allocated to each Australian university.[^cite_note-\:5-135] In response to the Albanese Government's announcement, the [Allen government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allan_government) in [Victoria](4689460.md) voiced concerns that the change could cost the [Victorian economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Victoria_\(state\)) \$5.9 billion by 2027.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] During a senate inquiry into the proposed legislation, representatives from the Australian university sector voiced similar concerns about the economic impact of the policy, and the consequent effects on long term tertiary education outcomes for both international and domestic students.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-\:5-135][^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141]

### Health

[Mark Butler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Butler) was appointed [Health Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Health_and_Aged_Care). During the 2022 election campaign, Labor committed \$135 million to fund 50 bulk-billed urgent care clinics, a policy designed to ease pressure on hospital emergency wards.[^cite_note-142] By the start of 2024, 58 clinics had opened across every state and territory of Australia.[^cite_note-143] Over the first six months of operation, more than 130,000 patients were treated for non-life-threatening medical emergencies.[^cite_note-144]

#### Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme

In October 2022, the government's cheaper medications bill passed the parliament. The new law reduced the cost of PBS medications by 29% and reduced the co-payment.[^cite_note-145]

#### Aged care

The first bill to pass the new Parliament was one responding to the 17 recommendations of the [Aged Care Royal Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_into_Aged_Care_Quality_and_Safety). The legislation amended the aged care funding model and introduces new reporting and transparency requirements.[^cite_note-auto3-146] The government also introduced a bill in the first sitting of the new parliament seeking to implement its election commitment to ensure that there is a nurse in nursing homes at all times. This bill was passed by the Parliament on 27 October 2022.[^cite_note-147] The government entered submissions to the Fair Work Commission supporting the case for a wage increase in the aged care industry and committed to funding any such increase. The Commission ordered a 15% increase and the government is expected to enter submissions related to the timing and implementation of this.[^cite_note-148]

#### NDIS

In mid-August 2022, the federal government announced that providers in the [National Disability Insurance Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Disability_Insurance_Scheme) would be scrutinised for fraud. The government also confirmed the establishment of a multiagency task force to look into this issue.[^cite_note-149] On 7 July 2024, the NDIS Minister [Bill Shorten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Shorten) confirmed that [sex work](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_work) would no longer be funded through the NDIS under planned reforms.[^cite_note-150] On 5 September 2024, Shorten confirmed that he would retire from politics and his ministerial portfolio in February 2025 in order to take up the position as vice-chancellor of the [University of Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Canberra).[^cite_note-151]

#### Immunisation

On 10 November 2024, health minister Mark Butler announced that pregnant women and newborn babies would have access to free [respiratory syncytial virus vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_syncytial_virus_vaccine) before winter 2025 under the [National Immunisation Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Immunisation_Program). In addition, babies would be eligible for national access to [monoclonal antibody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monoclonal_antibody).[^cite_note-152]

#### Medicare

On 22 February 2025, Albanese announced that the government would invest \$8.5 billion in [Medicare](58499.md) services including \$18 million in subsidised [general practitioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_practitioner)'s visits, 400 nursing scholarships and general practitioner training programs for 2,000 doctors.[^cite_note-153]

#### COVID-19 pandemic

On 30 June 2022, Butler ordered an inquiry into Australia's COVID-19 vaccine supplies, future variant jabs and how the rollout was caught short at the height of the pandemic. The review will also examine the deals entered into by the former Morrison government to purchase vaccines and therapeutic treatments.[^cite_note-154] Pandemic leave payments and access to free [rapid antigen tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_antigen_test) for concession card holders expired on 1 July 2022. The new government initially did not take steps to extend these programs, but reinstated them on 16 July 2022.[^cite_note-155] On 3 July 2022, Butler announced that from 12\:01 am on 6 July 2022 changes to the Biosecurity Act would come into effect which provide that persons seeking to visit Australia would no longer have to declare their COVID-19 vaccination status.[^cite_note-156]

On 7 July 2022, Butler confirmed that persons aged over 30 would be eligible to receive a fourth COVID-19 vaccine dose from 11 July 2022.[^cite_note-157] The government altered rules governing isolation periods so that persons infected with the virus only need isolate for 5 days from 9 September 2022.[^cite_note-158] Albanese announced on 30 September that a mandatory isolation period would no longer apply at all for persons infected with COVID-19.[^cite_note-159] On 21 September 2023, Albanese announced an independent inquiry into the federal government's handling of the [COVID-19 pandemic in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Australia), led by former public servant [Robyn Kruk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robyn_Kruk), infectious diseases expert Catherine Bennett and economist Angela Jackson.[^cite_note-160] The royal inquiry drew criticism from the [Australian Medical Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Medical_Association) and the [Human Rights Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Human_Rights_Commission) for excluding state and territorial governments from its scope.[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163]

### Energy and climate change



[Chris Bowen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bowen), current [Minister for Climate Change and Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Climate_Change_and_Energy)

[Chris Bowen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bowen) was appointed [Minister for Climate Change and Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Climate_Change_and_Energy) in the Albanese ministry. On 16 June 2022, Bowen and Albanese submitted a new Nationally Determined Contribution to the United Nations which formally committed Australia to reducing carbon emissions by 43% on 2005 levels. This represented an increase from the 26–28% reduction target submitted by the [Abbott government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbott_government).[^cite_note-164] The Albanese government also codified the new reduction target in legislation, which passed with support from the Greens and crossbench senators.[^cite_note-165]

In late 2022, the Albanese government announced reforms to the "safeguard mechanism", which requires Australia's largest carbon emitters to keep their emissions under a "baseline limit", either by reducing emissions or purchasing [carbon credits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_credit). The scheme was introduced by the [Turnbull government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turnbull_government) in 2016, but failed to reduce emissions as the rules were often left unenforced.[^cite_note-166] However, the Albanese amendments were met by opposition from the [Australian Greens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Greens), whose support was crucial in the Senate; Greens leader [Adam Bandt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Bandt) argued that the reforms did not go far enough.[^cite_note-167] The two parties reached an agreement on 27 March 2023, with the Greens negotiating a "hard cap" on emissions that cannot be offset by carbon credits.[^cite_note-168] The bill was passed on 30 March 2023, marking the most significant piece of climate change legislation passed through the Australian Parliament since 2011's *[Clean Energy Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_Energy_Act_2011)*.[^cite_note-169]

In late 2023, Australia re-joined the United Nations' [Green Climate Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Climate_Fund), from which the previous Morrison government had withdrawn in 2018.[^cite_note-170] As part of re-joining, the Albanese government committed \$150 million in climate finance to Pacific island countries.[^cite_note-171] In late March 2024, Albanese along with Bowen and industry minister [Ed Husic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Husic) travelled to the former [Liddell Power Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liddell_Power_Station) in the [Hunter Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Valley) to announce a \$1 billion solar panel program.[^cite_note-172] The visit later attracted criticism and accusations of hypocrisy after it was revealed that the ministers had travelled into the area on two separate private jets.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] In response to criticism, Bowen said that the [Royal Australian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Australian_Air_Force) had been responsible for their travel arrangements.[^cite_note-175]

#### Energy prices

In April 2022, shortly before the Albanese government took office, the independent [Australian Energy Regulator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Energy_Regulator) (AER) determined that the retail electricity pricing cap would be lifted on 1 July 2022 in response to higher wholesale prices.[^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177] Amidst the [global surge in energy prices](68934947.md), Australia entered into an energy crisis marked by significantly increased power prices.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] Bowen convened a meeting with his state and territory counterparts on 8 June 2022. The ministers agreed to expedite work on a capacity mechanism which will require the AER to pay retailers to maintain excess capacity.[^cite_note-180]

On 9 December 2022, Albanese announced that [National Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cabinet_\(Australia\)) had agreed to a co-ordinated plan to introduce temporary caps on gas and coal prices.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] The Bill passed the federal Parliament on 15 December 2022, creating a 12-month cap on energy prices and a \$1.5 billion relief package for households and businesses.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] In May 2024, as part of the [Albanese government's third budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Australian_federal_budget), every Australian household received a \$300 credit on their energy bill via four quarterly instalments of \$75.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] In May 2025, as part of its [fourth budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Australian_federal_budget), the government added a further \$150 credit by extending the instalments by another two quarters.[^cite_note-187]

#### Electric vehicles

In July 2022, the Albanese government introduced a bill to exempt certain [electric vehicles](279350.md) from [fringe benefits tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fringe_benefits_tax_\(Australia\)). This passed Parliament in November 2022.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] In March 2024, the Albanese government introduced a bill to apply a [vehicle emission standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_emission_standard) to new vehicles sold in Australia from 1 July 2025.[^cite_note-190] The Parliament passed this [New Vehicle Efficiency Standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Vehicle_Efficiency_Standard) in May 2024.[^cite_note-191] Albanese said that electric vehicles "can do anything that petrol vehicles can do, they'll do it in a way which is more efficient, that boosts productivity, that saves money, as well as driving down emissions".[^cite_note-192]

### Environment



[Tanya Plibersek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanya_Plibersek), current [Minister for the Environment and Water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_the_Environment_and_Water_\(Australia\)), speaking in late 2013

[Tanya Plibersek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanya_Plibersek) was appointed [Minister for the Environment and Water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_the_Environment_and_Water_\(Australia\)). Plibersek attended the UN ocean conference in Lisbon on 26 June 2022 where she announced five new blue carbon projects which are understood to include assistance for developing nations to safeguard the health of their oceans.[^cite_note-193] On 19 July 2022, Plibersek released the State of the Environment Report which had been handed to the previous government in December 2021. The Report provided that every category of the Australian environment – apart from urban environments – was now in a poor and deteriorating state. In response, Plibersek announced that the government would adopt a new target of protecting 30% of the Australian environment and promised stronger environmental protection legislation to be introduced in 2023.[^cite_note-194]

On 8 December 2022, Plibersek announced that the government would commit to a reform of federal environmental laws, in response to an independent review first submitted to the Morrison government in 2020. The reform would include the creation of a federal Environment Protection Agency (EPA) which would impose legally binding environmental standards and oversee decision-making processes of the [states and territories](4689096.md), as well as increased restrictions to [native logging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old-growth_forest#Logging_in_old-growth_forests) and the establishment of a "traffic light" rating system where some areas could be designated as having a high conservation value. The government planned to introduce legislation to Parliament before the end of 2023.[^cite_note-195] On 29 May 2024, Plibersek introduced to Parliament a modified version of the reforms.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] In mid-May 2024, the [Federal Court of Australia](4688076.md) ruled Plibersek in her capacity as federal environment minister did not need to consider the environmental impacts of emissions when approving gas or coal projects.[^cite_note-198]

#### Expansion of marine parks

On 5 June 2023, Plibersek announced that the government would triple the size of the [Macquarie Island Marine Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macquarie_Island_Marine_Park), with the vast majority of the park being completely closed to fishing, mining, and other extractive activities.[^cite_note-199] This expansion came into effect on 1 July 2023.[^cite_note-200] On 8 October 2024, Plibersek announced that the government would quadruple the size of the [Heard and McDonald Islands](13383.md) Marine Park, representing an increase of 310,000 square kilometres in protected area of restricted fishing.[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202] This expansion came into effect on 24 January 2025 and means that over half of Australia's ocean area is under protection.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204]

### Indigenous affairs



[Linda Burney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Burney), [Minister for Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Indigenous_Australians) from 2022 to 2024

At Albanese's first press conference as Prime Minister, the podium flags in the blue room at Parliament were changed to include Indigenous and Torres Strait Islander flags in addition to the Australian flag.[^cite_note-205] Upon the opening of the [new Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/47th_Parliament_of_Australia), both flags began to be displayed in the [House of Representatives](61565.md) and [Senate](165705.md) chambers.[^cite_note-206] [Linda Burney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Burney) was sworn in as [Minister for Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Indigenous_Affairs) on 1 June 2022. She was the first Indigenous woman to serve in the role.[^cite_note-207] In July 2024, following Minister Burney's retirement from the Cabinet, her Assistant Minister [Malarndirri McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malarndirri_McCarthy) became the new Minister for Indigenous Australians.[^cite_note-208] Disparity in health and socio-economic outcomes between First Nations peoples and non-Indigenous, remained an issue under the Albanese Government. In February 2024, data released by the Australian Productivity Commission indicating that only five of 19 target health and socioeconomic outcomes were on track to reach parity by the intended 2031 deadline.[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210]

#### Uluru Statement from the Heart

When declaring victory on election night, Albanese confirmed that his government was committed to implementing the [Uluru Statement from the Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uluru_Statement_from_the_Heart) in full within its first term.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-\:1-212] On 30 July 2022, Albanese attended the [Garma Festival of Traditional Cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garma_Festival_of_Traditional_Cultures), where he announced the proposed question the government intended to put to a referendum for the inclusion of an [Indigenous Voice to Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Voice_to_Parliament) in the Australian Constitution: "Do you support an alteration to the Constitution that establishes an Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Voice?"[^cite_note-213]

On 3 February 2023, all first ministers signed a statement of intent through the National Cabinet committing to support constitutional recognition of a Voice to Parliament.[^cite_note-214] A [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Australian_Indigenous_Voice_referendum) on an [Indigenous Voice to Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Voice_to_Parliament) was held on the 14 October 2023 and was rejected nationally.[^cite_note-\:02-215] The Yes23 campaign co-chair [Rachel Perkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Perkins) called for a week of silence "to grieve this outcome and reflect on its meaning and significance".[^cite_note-216] On 3 August 2024, Albanese announced his government no longer intended to pursue Truth and Treaty through the establishment of a formal Makaratta Commission.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218]

### Internet and social media

#### Minimum age for social media access

Main article: [Online Safety Amendment](78404606.md)

In May 2024, the federal government allocated \$6.5 million from the [2024 Australian federal budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Australian_federal_budget) to a pilot age verification scheme meant to protect children from accessing pornography and other harmful digital content in response to a sharp rise in domestic violence nationally.[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] On 10 September 2024, Albanese and [Minister for Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Communications) [Michelle Rowland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Rowland) confirmed that the federal government would introduce legislation to enforce a minimum age for access to social media and other relevant digital platforms. Albanese said that the legislation was intended to safeguard the safety and mental and physical health of young people, while Rowland said that the proposed legislation would hold [big tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tech) to account for harmful online environments and social media addiction among children.[^cite_note-221] The federal government's announcement followed [South Australia](26716.md)'s plan to restrict social media access to people aged 14 and above, and the [Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_\(Australia\))'s promise to restrict social media access to people aged under 16 if it won the [2025 Australian federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Australian_federal_election).[^cite_note-222]

In response, the Australian Association of Psychologists director Carly Dober described the proposal as a "bandaid response to a very complicated and deeply entrenched issue".[^cite_note-1_News_social_media_ban-223] The Australian Internet regulator, the eSafety Commissioner, who expressed concern that a social media ban would exclude young people from "meaningful" digital engagement and access to critical support.[^cite_note-Reuters_social_media_ban-224] By contrast, the 36Months initiative has supported the social media age limit on the grounds that excessive social media usage was "rewiring young brains" and causing an "epidemic of mental illness".[^cite_note-225]

On 7 November 2024, Albanese confirmed that the minimum age would be 16 and would not include exemptions for young people who already have social media accounts or those with [parental consent](8314613.md).[^cite_note-226] On 21 November, the Albanese government introduced the legislation with proposed fines of up to A\$49.5 million (US\$32 million) on social media platforms for systemic breaches. The proposed law would affect [Facebook](7529378.md), [Instagram](31591547.md), [TikTok](56822861.md), [X (formerly Twitter)](9988187.md) and [Snapchat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snapchat). However, Albanese confirmed that children would still have access to messaging, online gaming, and health and education-related services including the youth mental health platform [Headspace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Headspace_\(company\)), [Google Classroom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Classroom) and [YouTube](3524766.md), [Messenger Kids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messenger_Kids), [WhatsApp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WhatsApp) and [Kids Helpline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kids_Helpline).[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-AP_29_Nov_2024-228] On 29 November 2024, the Australian Parliament passed the [legislation](78404606.md) with support from the [Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_\(Australia\)) but opposition from the Australian Greens.[^cite_note-AP_29_Nov_2024-228] In response, tech companies Meta Platforms, TikTok and Google criticised the age verification legislation but indicated they would work with the government to implement the legislation over the next 12 months.[^cite_note-229]

On 26 November 2025, the Digital Freedom Project announced it would commence legal action in the High Court against the new laws, saying they violate the [implied right to political communication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_political_communication) in the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Australia).[^cite_note-Evans-230]

#### Social media regulation

Following the [2024 Wakeley church stabbing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Wakeley_church_stabbing) on 15 April 2024, the federal government ordered [Meta Platforms](62420226.md) and X (formerly [Twitter](9988187.md)) to remove offensive content relating to the attack within 24 hours or face fines.[^cite_note-231] In response, X announced it would challenge the federal government's decision in court, with its owner [Elon Musk](909036.md) accusing the government of [censorship](11056386.md).[^cite_note-232] The [Federal Court of Australia](4688076.md) said that it was unreasonable for the eSafety Commission to require X to remove access to the video globally and noted that a block would be ignored by other countries.[^cite_note-233] The eSafety Commissioner [Julie Inman Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julie_Inman_Grant) dropped the Federal Court case on 5 June 2024, but stated that she would continue legal action in the [Administrative Appeals Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_Appeals_Tribunal).[^cite_note-234]

On 12 September 2024, the federal government introduced legislation to combat social media misinformation. It would allow the [Australian Communications and Media Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Communications_and_Media_Authority) (ACMA) to require social media platforms to set internal industry standards around serious harm to election integrity, public health, incitement of hatred and violence, and disruption of key infrastructure or emergency services.[^cite_note-235] The ACMA would have the power to fine social media companies up to five percent of their annual revenue for non-compliance.[^cite_note-236] In response, X's owner Musk denounced the Australian government as "fascists". Government Services Minister [Bill Shorten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Shorten) accused Musk of being disingenuous about free speech, stating, "When it's in its commercial interests, he \[Elon Musk] is the champion of free speech and when he doesn't like it... he's going to shut it all down."[^cite_note-237] On 25 November 2024, the Albanese government withdrew the proposed legislation amid criticism over its impact on free speech.[^cite_note-238]

#### Ban on TikTok on government devices

On 4 April 2023, the Albanese government banned the video streaming app [TikTok](56822861.md) on all government devices, including the mobile phones of politicians.[^cite_note-239] Prior to the Australian federal government's ban, 68 federal agencies had banned TikTok on work-related mobile devices by early March 2023.[^cite_note-240] The federal government subsequently began a review of TikTok on 21 March 2023.[^cite_note-241]

#### Ban on doxxing and non-consensual deepfake pornography

In June 2024, the Albanese government introduced legislation to criminalise the creation and sharing of non-consensual [deepfake pornography](62590682.md).[^cite_note-242] The legislation passed the Parliament two months later with broad political support.[^cite_note-243] In November 2024, the Albanese government passed legislation that criminalised [doxxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doxing).[^cite_note-244]

### Education

[Jason Clare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Clare) was appointed [Minister for Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Education_\(Australia\)). On 17 June 2022, Clare confirmed that the Albanese government intends to make changes which give schools a choice of whether to hire a religious or secular pastoral care worker through the [National School Chaplaincy Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_School_Chaplaincy_Programme).[^cite_note-245] Clare announced an Australian Universities Accord with the terms of reference covering funding, affordability, employment conditions for staff, and how universities and [TAFEs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TAFE) can work together. The Accord's final report is due to be handed down in December 2023.[^cite_note-246] In November 2024, the Albanese government passed legislation to change the calculation of annual indexation on [student university debts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education_fees_in_Australia). Previously it was based on the [consumer price index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_price_index) (CPI), with the change making it determined by what is lower between the CPI or the wage price index (wage growth). The change in indexation removed an estimated \$3 billion in Australian student debt loans.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248]

The 10-year anniversary of the Gonski report in May 2022 led to criticism of successive state governments failure to implement its recommendations.[^cite_note-10years-249] By the late March 2025 all states and territories had signed on to the new school funding agreement with the Albanese government lifting federal funding of public schools to 25 per cent from 20 per cent, under the Better and Fairer Schools Agreement (BFSA).[^cite_note-duffy2025-250][^cite_note-qld-251] Under the BFSA, states are required to increase their funding of public schools to 75 per cent of the minimum amount recommended by the 2012 [Gonski Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonski_Report) and [Gonski 2.0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonski_2.0), per the Schooling Resource Standard proposed by panel chair [David Gonski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Gonski).[^cite_note-qld-251][^cite_note-duffy2025-250] This means that they will be fully funded according to the Gonski model.[^cite_note-wilson2025-252]

#### Childcare

In November 2022, the parliament passed the government's cheaper childcare laws. The new laws commenced operation in July 2023 and increased the childcare subsidy from 85% to 90% for families on an income below \$80,000.[^cite_note-253]

### Housing



[Julie Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julie_Collins), current [Minister for Housing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Housing_\(Australia\))

[Julie Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julie_Collins) was appointed [Minister for Housing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Housing_\(Australia\)),[^cite_note-254] in the midst of a [housing affordability crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_crisis).[^cite_note-255] On 1 October 2022, the government launched its Regional First Home Buyer Guarantee. The program provides a government guarantee of up to 15 per cent for eligible first home buyers, so regional Australians with a deposit of as little as 5 per cent can avoid paying lenders’ mortgage insurance.[^cite_note-256] In August 2023, Albanese reached an agreement with [National Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cabinet_\(Australia\)) to build 1.2 million houses over five years.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] On 13 September 2023, the government's Housing Australia Future Fund passed the Parliament. This is a \$10 billion investment fund controlled by the [Future Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_Fund) that is designed to build 30,000 new social and affordable homes over the following five years in a further effort to increase supply.[^cite_note-259]

On 25 September 2024, the Albanese government instructed the [Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treasury_\(Australia\)) to examine [negative gearing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negative_gearing). As a divisive [wedge issue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wedge_issue) in Australian politics, changes to negative gearing rules were proposed by Labor prior to the 2019 election, in which they were defeated. Albanese had previously pledged not to modify negative gearing as Opposition Leader, leading to criticism from the Coalition and conservative media outlets for appearing to backtrack on this promise.[^cite_note-260] After intense media speculation, Albanese ruled out making changes to negative gearing the next day.[^cite_note-261] In November 2024, the Albanese government legislated a "help-to-buy" [shared equity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shared_equity) scheme that aims to allow up to 40,000 first-time home buyers to purchase a home with a shared contribution with the government, and a tax concession to incentivise developers to build houses [specifically for the purpose of renting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_to_rent).[^cite_note-262]

### Welfare

The Albanese government announced that existing mutual obligation penalties would be expunged from people's records as the government transitioned to the [Workforce Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workforce_Australia) system for [JobSeeker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_security_in_Australia), saying that it was "too late" to scrap the system.[^cite_note-263] A review of the Workforce Australia JobSeeker scheme will be tabled in parliament in September 2023.[^cite_note-264] Legislation to end the [Cashless Debit Card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cashless_Welfare_Card) was passed by the House of Representatives, and will be considered by the Senate in September.[^cite_note-265] Cashless Debit Card users in the Northern Territory will be transitioned back onto the BasicsCard, a Howard-era income management scheme,[^cite_note-266] despite Labor promising prior to the election to end compulsory income management.[^cite_note-267] A [Royal Commission into the Robodebt Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_into_the_Robodebt_Scheme) was announced by Albanese with Letters Patent issued on 25 August 2022. The Royal Commission will be chaired by former [Queensland Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Queensland) [Justice Catherine Holmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Holmes) and is expected to conclude on 18 April 2023.[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269] The Albanese government agreed to all recommendations of the royal commission, either in full or in principle, but rejected a recommendation about the freedom of information act.[^cite_note-270]

### Justice



[Attorney-General of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Australia) [Mark Dreyfus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Dreyfus) in 2015

#### National Anti-Corruption Commission

Main article: [National Anti-Corruption Commission (Australia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Anti-Corruption_Commission_\(Australia\))

During the election campaign, Albanese confirmed that his government would seek to establish a federal National Anti-Corruption Commission within its first year in office.[^cite_note-Eight_priorities-271] On the day he was sworn in, Albanese confirmed that he had already ordered work to begin on this task.[^cite_note-272] Attorney-General [Mark Dreyfus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Dreyfus) said that the government's legislation would include provision for the commission to investigate [pork-barrelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pork_barrel), as well as "serious and systemic" past corruption allegations.[^cite_note-273] In September 2022, Dreyfus introduced a bill to establish a National Anti-Corruption Commission (NACC).[^cite_note-274] The bill passed the Parliament on 30 November 2022 in line with the government's commitment to establish an anti-corruption commission prior to the end of 2022.[^cite_note-275] The NACC came into existence on 1 July 2023.[^cite_note-276]

#### Administrative Appeals Tribunal

On 16 December 2022, the Labor Albanese government announced that it will abolish the [Administrative Appeals Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_Appeals_Tribunal) (AAT) and replace it with a new body, claiming that the AAT had been "fatally compromised" by political appointments and "cronyism".[^cite_note-277] On 28 May 2024, the legislation to establish the AAT's replacement, the [Administrative Review Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_Review_Tribunal) (ART), passed the Parliament.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] The new Tribunal commenced in October 2024.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281]

#### Ban on public display of terrorist or Nazi symbols

In June 2023, the Albanese government introduced legislation to ban the public display of terrorist or Nazi symbols, including the [Nazi salute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_salute).[^cite_note-282] The ban passed Parliament in December 2023 and came into effect the following month.[^cite_note-283]

#### Bernard Collaery prosecution

On 7 July 2022, Attorney-General Mark Dreyfus exercised his power under section 71 of the *[Judiciary Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_Act_1903)* to cease proceedings against [Bernard Collaery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Collaery) in connection with the [Australia–East Timor spying scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93East_Timor_spying_scandal).[^cite_note-284]

#### Release of Julian Assange

Main article: [Julian Assange § Plea bargain and release](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_Assange#Plea_bargain_and_release)

In 2022, the incoming Albanese government indicated that it opposed the continued prosecution of [Julian Assange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_Assange) but intended to pursue quiet diplomacy to prevent it.[^cite_note-285] On 14 August 2023, US ambassador to Australia [Caroline Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Kennedy) flagged a potential plea deal for Assange,[^cite_note-286] shortly after meeting with a group of Australian parliamentarians pushing for Assange's return.[^cite_note-287] On 24 June 2024, Assange agreed to a [plea bargain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plea_bargain) with the US Department, in which Assange would plead guilty to one felony count of violating the Espionage Act in exchange for immediate release.[^cite_note-plea-288] The agreement entailed the US Department of Justice seeking a sentence of 62 months, the time he had served in British prison while awaiting extradition.[^cite_note-release-289]

After release from British prison, Assange immediately flew via charter flight – with the accompaniment of his legal representatives and the Australian high commissioner to the United Kingdom, [Stephen Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Smith_\(Australian_politician\)) – to [Saipan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saipan,_Northern_Mariana_Islands) to the [District Court for the Northern Mariana Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_Court_for_the_Northern_Mariana_Islands).[^cite_note-aupmconference-290] After arrival, Assange and Smith were joined by the Australian Ambassador to the United States, [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd).[^cite_note-291] Judge [Ramona Villagomez Manglona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramona_Villagomez_Manglona) accepted Assange's guilty plea and sentenced him to 62 months' time served.[^cite_note-pleaandsentence-292] Assange then returned to the charter flight and flew to Canberra.[^cite_note-pleaUpdate-293] Assange was required by the Australian government to repay the costs of the charter flight, requested at US\$520,000, as he was not permitted to fly on commercial airlines.[^cite_note-294] Upon arrival in Australia, Albanese phoned Assange. Assange's lawyer later recounted that Assange had said that Albanese had "saved his life" and that Rudd and Smith were the "diplomatic A-team".[^cite_note-295]

### Agriculture, fisheries and forestry

[Murray Watt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_Watt) was sworn in as [Minister for Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Agriculture,_Fisheries_and_Forestry) in late May 2022.[^cite_note-296] Following a cabinet reshuffle in July 2024, he was succeeded by Julie Collins.[^cite_note-297] On 11 May 2024, Watt announced that [Western Australia](33613.md)'s [live sheep export trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_live_export_industry) would end from 1 May 2028.[^cite_note-sheep-298] While [RSPCA Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSPCA_Australia) welcomed the move, the policy announcement was condemned by [Nationals](21927.md) leader [David Littleproud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Littleproud), Western Australian opposition leader [Shane Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shane_Love), [National Farmers' Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Farmers'_Federation) CEO Tony Maher and WA Livestock president Geoff Pearson.[^cite_note-sheep-298][^cite_note-299] Western Australian premier [Roger Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Cook_\(politician\)) also criticised the Government's support package for farmers as insufficient.[^cite_note-300] In late May 2024, the Western Australian agricultural sector launched a "Keep the Sheep" campaign in opposition to the federal government's decision to end live sheep exports by 2028.[^cite_note-301] Campaigners staged a large protest rally in [Perth](24355.md), which disrupted traffic.[^cite_note-302]

### Disaster relief

#### 2022 floods response

Following the [2022 New South Wales floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_New_South_Wales_floods) in July 2022, the government announced one-off, non-means tested disaster relief payments to persons living in 29 local government areas impacted by the disaster.[^cite_note-303] On 12 July 2022, Albanese announced a further \$80 million to assist with clean-up efforts as well as grants for farmers, small businesses, not-for-profit organisations and local councils. Albanese also announced \$36 million for a program to assess buildings and flooded properties including free demolition of those found to be unlivable.[^cite_note-304] Following the [2022 south eastern Australia floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_south_eastern_Australia_floods), the federal government made a one-off, non-means tested disaster recovery payment of \$1,000 per eligible adult and \$400 per eligible child available.[^cite_note-vfc-305] Across the states suffering in the crisis, 23 local government areas were made eligible.[^cite_note-aci-306]

#### 2024 Victorian flood

On 10 January 2024, Albanese announced that the federal government would provide support to victims of the [Murchison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murchison,_Victoria) flooding.[^cite_note-307]

#### 2024 Port Vila earthquake

In response to the [2024 Port Vila earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Port_Vila_earthquake) that occurred on 17 December 2024, the Albanese government dispatched military personnel, urban search and rescue teams, firefighters, paramedics, volunteer engineers, canine handlers and medical personnel to [Port Vila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Vila), [Vanuatu](32443.md) to assist with local rescue and recovery efforts.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309] The Australian government also assisted with efforts to reopen Port Vila's airport.[^cite_note-310] The [Royal Australian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Australian_Air_Force) evacuated 148 Australian citizens from Vanuatu.[^cite_note-311]

#### Protection of cash usage

On 17 November 2024, the Albanese government confirmed that it would mandate that businesses be required to accept [cash](55336.md) [payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payment) for essential items from 2026 to ensure that Australians who rely on cash including during natural disasters or digital outages can continue to make purchases.[^cite_note-312]

### Other domestic policy

#### Parliamentary affairs and ethics

[Tony Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Burke) was appointed [Leader of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_\(Australia\)) in the Albanese ministry. Prior to being sworn in, Burke said that he was determined to "fix" parliamentary procedures and noted that the situation had become a "farce" during the [previous Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/46th_Parliament_of_Australia).[^cite_note-313] On 24 June 2022, Prime Minister Albanese decided to cut [crossbench](361590.md) advisory staff from 4 to the pre-Coalition level of 1. This decision worsened the government's relations with the Senate crossbench and the [Teal independents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teal_independents),[^cite_note-314] with many crossbench Parliamentarians later having their staff allocations increased after making direct appeals to Albanese.[^cite_note-315] On 7 July 2022, Albanese unveiled a new ministerial code of conduct which prohibited ministers from utilising blind trusts. This was in response to the blind trust used by [Christian Porter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Porter) under the previous government to fund personal defamation proceedings brought by him.[^cite_note-316]

On 26 August 2022, Albanese and Attorney-General [Mark Dreyfus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Dreyfus) announced that the government had appointed former High Court Justice [Virginia Bell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Bell_\(judge\)) to lead an inquiry into the appointment of former prime minister, the Hon [Scott Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Morrison) MP, to administer departments other than the Department of Prime Minister and Cabinet and related matters".[^cite_note-317] Bell found that Morrison's appointments were corrosive of public trust in government and recommended the implementation of legislation requiring the public announcement of ministerial appointments.[^cite_note-318] Albanese confirmed that he would recommend that his cabinet implement all of Bell's recommendations in this regard[^cite_note-319] and Burke successfully moved a motion in the House censuring Morrison on 30 November 2022, making him the first former prime minister to be censured.[^cite_note-320]

#### 2026 Australian census

On 30 August 2024, Albanese confirmed during an interview with [ABC Radio Melbourne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_Radio_Melbourne) that the [2026 Australian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Australian_census) would include a question relating to sexuality and gender identity, reversing an earlier statement that his government had abandoned the proposal.[^cite_note-321] The following day, Albanese denied that the federal government had revised its policy regarding the inclusion of a gender identity and sexuality question in the upcoming 2026 census.[^cite_note-322] On 8 September, federal treasurer Jim Chalmers confirmed that the 2026 census would include questions about sexual orientation and gender.[^cite_note-323]

#### Territory rights

[Kristy McBain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristy_McBain) was appointed Minister for the Territories. In June 2022, she confirmed that the government intended to introduce a bill to give the [Northern Territory](21638.md) and the [Australian Capital Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Capital_Territory) the right to enact their own voluntary-assisted dying laws. However, she noted that the government had no plans to expand the number of senators representing the Australian Capital Territory despite its rapidly expanding population.[^cite_note-324] On 1 December 2022, the government's bill to empower the Territories to make laws relating to voluntary-assisted dying passed the Parliament.[^cite_note-325]

#### Republic

[Matt Thistlethwaite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Thistlethwaite) was appointed the first [Assistant Minister for the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Australia) on 1 June 2022. Thistlethwaite confirmed that the government's priority during its first term would be to [seek constitutional recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Australian_Indigenous_Voice_referendum) of Indigenous Australians, but that a transition to a republic could be on the agenda for a potential second term.[^cite_note-326] Following the death of Queen [Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_II) on 8 September 2022, Albanese reiterated that his government would not pursue becoming a republic during their first parliamentary term.[^cite_note-327] In July 2024, the role of Assistant Minister for the Republic was abolished.[^cite_note-328]

Albanese later confirmed a republic referendum will not be held during his prime ministership.[^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330]

#### Australia Day

On 16 December 2022, the Albanese government removed a [Morrison government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morrison_government) requirement for local councils to hold citizenship ceremonies on 26 January, [Australia Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_Day).[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] On 18 January 2023, the Albanese government removed a Morrison government ban on federal public servants working on Australia Day.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335]

#### Culture

On 30 January 2023, Albanese unveiled a new national cultural policy "Revive" with \$286 million in funding over four years, labelled the most significant investment in [Australian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_culture) since the Keating government, with a focus on literature, music, [cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Australia) and [television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Australia), and [First Nations](12598742.md) language and culture.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338]

#### Gender equity in sports

In early September 2024, the Albanese government launched its [National Gender Equity in Sports Governance Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gender_Equity_in_Sports_Governance_Policy?action=edit\&redlink=1) to boost the representation of women in governance and leadership positions in Australian sports by creating a financial initiative for sporting bodies to make their boards more equal. Under the policy, national and state-level sporting bodies across Australia must have an equal split of men and women on their boards by 1 July 2027 or risk having their funding suspended. This policy was developed in collaboration by the Albanese government, the [Australian Sports Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Sports_Commission) (ASC), and the various State and Territory Agencies for Sport and Recreation.[^cite_note-339]

### Foreign affairs, trade and defence



[Penny Wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Wong) and [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken) at the Quadrilateral Security Dialogue meeting

[Penny Wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Wong) was sworn in as [Minister for Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Foreign_Affairs_\(Australia\)) on 23 May 2022 as part of the interim Albanese ministry.
